PANTHEON REGISTRY — FIGURE-AUTHORING TRANSCRIPT
Tradition: Chinese (round 2 — the incomplete sets)
Eras (registered, exact): primordial; mythic; mythic-prehistory; xia; shang; zhou; spring-autumn; ...
Author pass: the Chinese key held 98 records. This adds 32.

================================================================================
THE GAP — NOT MISSING FIGURES, MISSING PARTS OF SETS
================================================================================

The Chinese key is one of the corpus's largest at **98 records**, and by the
usual measure it looks well covered: all eight Immortals, the whole Journey to
the West cast, the Three Pure Ones, the Fengshen Yanyi principals.

**The gap is not a list of absent names. It is that the sets are broken.**

    The Ten Kings of Diyu    corpus held 2 of 10  (Qinguang, Yanluo)
    The Four Dragon Kings    corpus held 1 of 4   (Ao Guang)
    The storm quartet        corpus held 1 of 4   (Leigong)
    The Three Officials      corpus held 0 of 3

And the registry **says so in its own data**. `chinese_yanluo_wang`'s record
already reads "fifth of the Ten Kings of Hell" — the corpus named a set of ten
inside a record and held two of them. That is the lane-30/34 pattern again: the
corpus writing down the very thing it does not hold.

The sharpest single case is **Leigong without Dianmu**. Leigong is 雷公,
"thunder LORD"; Dianmu is 電母, "lightning MOTHER". The sources are explicit that
gong and mu mark the yang and yin halves of one storm. **The two names encode each
other**, and the corpus held one of them. This programme has now found the
held-one-of-a-pair fault in twenty-two traditions, and this is its most literal
instance yet.

Below the top of the hierarchy, the corpus had nothing at all: **no Tudigong**,
the god of the shrine at the end of the street and the most worshipped deity in
China, and **no Chenghuang**, the City God. A celestial bureaucracy recorded only
from the Jade Emperor down to the Immortals is not a bureaucracy.

================================================================================
THE SWEEP — ONE HIT, AND IT WAS AN ALIAS OF A HELD FIGURE
================================================================================

Fifty candidate names swept against the whole corpus returned **exactly one hit**:
`Marshal Tianpeng` resolves to `chinese_zhu_bajie`. That is correct and it is not
a gap — Marshal Tianpeng is Zhu Bajie's rank *before* his fall and expulsion to
earth, so the name is an alias of a record the corpus already holds. It is
censused REJECT on exactly that ground.

Forty-nine of fifty candidates were absent outright, which is what a broken set
looks like from the outside: not a near-miss, just nothing there.

================================================================================
BLOCKS 1-2 — THE EIGHT MISSING KINGS OF THE TEN COURTS
================================================================================

A soul passes the courts **in order**, and each king's jurisdiction is defined by
what the others do not handle — which is why they cannot be authored singly:

    2  Chujiang   corrupt officials, dishonest doctors      the Mountain of Ice
    3  Songdi     ungrateful children, family discord       the heart torn out
    4  Wuguan     tax evaders, cheats, wasters of food      millstones, hot pillars
    6  Biancheng  desecrators of texts, blasphemers         the saw, the boulder
    7  Taishan    grave robbers, violators of burial        steaming, disembowelling
    8  Dushi      filial impiety, examination fraud         Hot Suffocation Hell
    9  Pingdeng   whatever the first eight missed           the Iron Web
   10  Zhuanlun   nobody — he assigns the next life         the Wheel of Rebirth

Two are worth pausing on. **Pingdeng Wang is an auditor**: his court exists to
catch clerical error in the other eight, which makes the Chinese hell the only
underworld in this corpus with a quality-control department. And **Zhuanlun Wang
punishes no one** — the Tenth Court only assigns the next life, and it is where
Meng Po, whom the corpus already holds, serves her Tea of Forgetfulness. The
corpus had the tea-bearer and not the king of the court she stands in.

**Wuguan Wang ships with no Chinese form.** The first element is written both 五官
and 仵官 in the sources and no single form is settled, so none is supplied.

     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the individual Courts of Diyu: the SECOND COURT under Chujiang Wang punishes corrupt officials, dishonest doctors and those who harmed others through deception, and its sinners face the Mountain of Ice, where they freeze in eternal cold. The THIRD COURT under Songdi Wang deals with ungrateful children, those who disrespected elders, and people who incited discord within families, and its punishment involves having one's heart torn out repeatedly, symbolising the pain they caused their families. The FOURTH COURT under Wuguan Wang punishes tax evaders, business cheats and those who wasted food, who are ground between millstones or forced to embrace red-hot metal pillars. The SIXTH COURT under Biancheng Wang punishes those who desecrated sacred texts, complained against heaven, misused religious teachings or showed disrespect to the Sanbao, and its sinners are sawn in half or crushed under boulders. The SEVENTH COURT under Taishan Wang deals with grave robbers, those who sold or consumed human flesh, and violators of burial customs, whose punishments include being steamed alive or having the intestines pulled out. The EIGHTH COURT, called Hot Suffocation Hell, is ruled by Dushi Wang and judges crimes of filial impiety, with sixteen sub-hells for those who caused their parents suffering; King Dushi also sentences cheating during examinations and harming others to benefit oneself. The NINTH COURT, called Iron Web, is ruled by Pingdeng Wang, who is the final checkpoint before rebirth: his job is to catch anything the previous eight courts missed, reviewing the entire case file and checking for clerical errors. The TENTH COURT, the Wheel of Rebirth, is ruled by Zhuanlun Wang, literally 'King of the Turning Wheel', who does not punish anyone but assigns the next life; Lady Meng Po gives the Tea of Forgetfulness there, which erases the memory of the punishments, and souls then pass through the Wheel of Reincarnation to be reborn as human or animal according to the past life."

      "reference": "Reference treatment of the individual Courts of Diyu: the SECOND COURT under Chujiang Wang punishes corrupt officials, dishonest doctors and those who harmed others through deception, and its sinners face the Mountain of Ice, where they freeze in eternal cold. The THIRD COURT under Songdi Wang deals with ungrateful children, those who disrespected elders, and people who incited discord within families, and its punishment involves having one's heart torn out repeatedly, symbolising the pain they caused their families. The FOURTH COURT under Wuguan Wang punishes tax evaders, business cheats and those who wasted food, who are ground between millstones or forced to embrace red-hot metal pillars. The SIXTH COURT under Biancheng Wang punishes those who desecrated sacred texts, complained against heaven, misused religious teachings or showed disrespect to the Sanbao, and its sinners are sawn in half or crushed under boulders. The SEVENTH COURT under Taishan Wang deals with grave robbers, those who sold or consumed human flesh, and violators of burial customs, whose punishments include being steamed alive or having the intestines pulled out. The EIGHTH COURT, called Hot Suffocation Hell, is ruled by Dushi Wang and judges crimes of filial impiety, with sixteen sub-hells for those who caused their parents suffering; King Dushi also sentences cheating during examinations and harming others to benefit oneself. The NINTH COURT, called Iron Web, is ruled by Pingdeng Wang, who is the final checkpoint before rebirth: his job is to catch anything the previous eight courts missed, reviewing the entire case file and checking for clerical errors. The TENTH COURT, the Wheel of Rebirth, is ruled by Zhuanlun Wang, literally 'King of the Turning Wheel', who does not punish anyone but assigns the next life; Lady Meng Po gives the Tea of Forgetfulness there, which erases the memory of the punishments, and souls then pass through the Wheel of Reincarnation to be reborn as human or animal according to the past life."
     }
    ]
   }

   {
    "sphereId": "judgement-of-the-dead",
    "contextTag": "lifelong",
    "sources": [
     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the Ten Courts of Diyu: the concept of the Ten Courts of Yanluo began after Chinese folk religion was influenced by Buddhism; hell is divided into ten courts, each ruled by a king who judges the dead for their earthly sins before sentencing punishment, and the ten are collectively the Ten Yama Kings, operating like imperial judges supported by clerks who maintain detailed registers of human actions. They are, in order: the first hall's King Qinguang, the second hall's King Chujiang, the third hall's King Songdi, the fourth hall's King Wuguan, the fifth hall's King Yama (Yanluo Wang), the sixth hall's King Biancheng, the seventh hall's King Taishan, the eighth hall's King Dushi, the ninth hall's King Pingdeng, and the tenth hall's King of the Turning Wheel (Zhuanlun Wang). Yanluo Wang rules the Fifth Court and is the most famous of the ten."

      "reference": "Reference treatment of the individual Courts of Diyu: the SECOND COURT under Chujiang Wang punishes corrupt officials, dishonest doctors and those who harmed others through deception, and its sinners face the Mountain of Ice, where they freeze in eternal cold. The THIRD COURT under Songdi Wang deals with ungrateful children, those who disrespected elders, and people who incited discord within families, and its punishment involves having one's heart torn out repeatedly, symbolising the pain they caused their families. The FOURTH COURT under Wuguan Wang punishes tax evaders, business cheats and those who wasted food, who are ground between millstones or forced to embrace red-hot metal pillars. The SIXTH COURT under Biancheng Wang punishes those who desecrated sacred texts, complained against heaven, misused religious teachings or showed disrespect to the Sanbao, and its sinners are sawn in half or crushed under boulders. The SEVENTH COURT under Taishan Wang deals with grave robbers, those who sold or consumed human flesh, and violators of burial customs, whose punishments include being steamed alive or having the intestines pulled out. The EIGHTH COURT, called Hot Suffocation Hell, is ruled by Dushi Wang and judges crimes of filial impiety, with sixteen sub-hells for those who caused their parents suffering; King Dushi also sentences cheating during examinations and harming others to benefit oneself. The NINTH COURT, called Iron Web, is ruled by Pingdeng Wang, who is the final checkpoint before rebirth: his job is to catch anything the previous eight courts missed, reviewing the entire case file and checking for clerical errors. The TENTH COURT, the Wheel of Rebirth, is ruled by Zhuanlun Wang, literally 'King of the Turning Wheel', who does not punish anyone but assigns the next life; Lady Meng Po gives the Tea of Forgetfulness there, which erases the memory of the punishments, and souls then pass through the Wheel of Reincarnation to be reborn as human or animal according to the past life."

The EIGHTH COURT, called Hot Suffocation Hell, is ruled by Dushi Wang and judges crimes of filial impiety, with sixteen sub-hells for those who caused their parents suffering; King Dushi also sentences cheating during examinations and harming others to benefit oneself. The NINTH COURT, called Iron Web, is ruled by Pingdeng Wang, who is the final checkpoint before rebirth: his job is to catch anything the previous eight courts missed, reviewing the entire case file and checking for clerical errors. The TENTH COURT, the Wheel of Rebirth, is ruled by Zhuanlun Wang, literally 'King of the Turning Wheel', who does not punish anyone but assigns the next life; Lady Meng Po gives the Tea of Forgetfulness there, which erases the memory of the punishments, and souls then pass through the Wheel of Reincarnation to be reborn as human or animal according to the past life."
     }
    ],

    "sphereId": "the-millstone-and-the-pillar",
    "contextTag": "lifelong",
    "sources": [
     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the individual Courts of Diyu: the SECOND COURT under Chujiang Wang punishes corrupt officials, dishonest doctors and those who harmed others through deception, and its sinners face the Mountain of Ice, where they freeze in eternal cold. The THIRD COURT under Songdi Wang deals with ungrateful children, those who disrespected elders, and people who incited discord within families, and its punishment involves having one's heart torn out repeatedly, symbolising the pain they caused their families. The FOURTH COURT under Wuguan Wang punishes tax evaders, business cheats and those who wasted food, who are ground between millstones or forced to embrace red-hot metal pillars. The SIXTH COURT under Biancheng Wang punishes those who desecrated sacred texts, complained against heaven, misused religious teachings or showed disrespect to the Sanbao, and its sinners are sawn in half or crushed under boulders. The SEVENTH COURT under Taishan Wang deals with grave robbers, those who sold or consumed human flesh, and violators of burial customs, whose punishments include being steamed alive or having the intestines pulled out. The EIGHTH COURT, called Hot Suffocation Hell, is ruled by Dushi Wang and judges crimes of filial impiety, with sixteen sub-hells for those who caused their parents suffering; King Dushi also sentences cheating during examinations and harming others to benefit oneself. The NINTH COURT, called Iron Web, is ruled by Pingdeng Wang, who is the final checkpoint before rebirth: his job is to catch anything the previous eight courts missed, reviewing the entire case file and checking for clerical errors. The TENTH COURT, the Wheel of Rebirth, is ruled by Zhuanlun Wang, literally 'King of the Turning Wheel', who does not punish anyone but assigns the next life; Lady Meng Po gives the Tea of Forgetfulness there, which erases the memory of the punishments, and souls then pass through the Wheel of Reincarnation to be reborn as human or animal according to the past life."

    "contextTag": "lifelong",
    "sources": [
     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the Ten Courts of Diyu: the concept of the Ten Courts of Yanluo began after Chinese folk religion was influenced by Buddhism; hell is divided into ten courts, each ruled by a king who judges the dead for their earthly sins before sentencing punishment, and the ten are collectively the Ten Yama Kings, operating like imperial judges supported by clerks who maintain detailed registers of human actions. They are, in order: the first hall's King Qinguang, the second hall's King Chujiang, the third hall's King Songdi, the fourth hall's King Wuguan, the fifth hall's King Yama (Yanluo Wang), the sixth hall's King Biancheng, the seventh hall's King Taishan, the eighth hall's King Dushi, the ninth hall's King Pingdeng, and the tenth hall's King of the Turning Wheel (Zhuanlun Wang). Yanluo Wang rules the Fifth Court and is the most famous of the ten."

     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the Ten Courts of Diyu: the concept of the Ten Courts of Yanluo began after Chinese folk religion was influenced by Buddhism; hell is divided into ten courts, each ruled by a king who judges the dead for their earthly sins before sentencing punishment, and the ten are collectively the Ten Yama Kings, operating like imperial judges supported by clerks who maintain detailed registers of human actions. They are, in order: the first hall's King Qinguang, the second hall's King Chujiang, the third hall's King Songdi, the fourth hall's King Wuguan, the fifth hall's King Yama (Yanluo Wang), the sixth hall's King Biancheng, the seventh hall's King Taishan, the eighth hall's King Dushi, the ninth hall's King Pingdeng, and the tenth hall's King of the Turning Wheel (Zhuanlun Wang). Yanluo Wang rules the Fifth Court and is the most famous of the ten."
     }
    ],
    "notes": "Reciprocated within this batch. THE TEN ARE A SINGLE INSTITUTION, not ten unrelated judges: a soul passes through the courts in order, and each king's jurisdiction is defined by what the others do not handle."

   {
    "claim": "King of the sixth Court of Diyu, who judges those who desecrated sacred texts, complained against heaven, misused religious teachings or showed disrespect to the Sanbao; the court's punishment is being sawn in half or crushed under boulders.",
    "citations": [
     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the individual Courts of Diyu: the SECOND COURT under Chujiang Wang punishes corrupt officials, dishonest doctors and those who harmed others through deception, and its sinners face the Mountain of Ice, where they freeze in eternal cold. The THIRD COURT under Songdi Wang deals with ungrateful children, those who disrespected elders, and people who incited discord within families, and its punishment involves having one's heart torn out repeatedly, symbolising the pain they caused their families. The FOURTH COURT under Wuguan Wang punishes tax evaders, business cheats and those who wasted food, who are ground between millstones or forced to embrace red-hot metal pillars. The SIXTH COURT under Biancheng Wang punishes those who desecrated sacred texts, complained against heaven, misused religious teachings or showed disrespect to the Sanbao, and its sinners are sawn in half or crushed under boulders. The SEVENTH COURT under Taishan Wang deals with grave robbers, those who sold or consumed human flesh, and violators of burial customs, whose punishments include being steamed alive or having the intestines pulled out. The EIGHTH COURT, called Hot Suffocation Hell, is ruled by Dushi Wang and judges crimes of filial impiety, with sixteen sub-hells for those who caused their parents suffering; King Dushi also sentences cheating during examinations and harming others to benefit oneself. The NINTH COURT, called Iron Web, is ruled by Pingdeng Wang, who is the final checkpoint before rebirth: his job is to catch anything the previous eight courts missed, reviewing the entire case file and checking for clerical errors. The TENTH COURT, the Wheel of Rebirth, is ruled by Zhuanlun Wang, literally 'King of the Turning Wheel', who does not punish anyone but assigns the next life; Lady Meng Po gives the Tea of Forgetfulness there, which erases the memory of the punishments, and souls then pass through the Wheel of Reincarnation to be reborn as human or animal according to the past life."
     }
    ],
    "weight": "high"
   }
  ],
  "notes": "THE SIXTH OF TEN, AND THE CORPUS HELD TWO. chinese_yanluo_wang's own record already says he is 'fifth of the Ten Kings of Hell' — the registry named the set in its own data and held only the first and the fifth of it."

     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the individual Courts of Diyu: the SECOND COURT under Chujiang Wang punishes corrupt officials, dishonest doctors and those who harmed others through deception, and its sinners face the Mountain of Ice, where they freeze in eternal cold. The THIRD COURT under Songdi Wang deals with ungrateful children, those who disrespected elders, and people who incited discord within families, and its punishment involves having one's heart torn out repeatedly, symbolising the pain they caused their families. The FOURTH COURT under Wuguan Wang punishes tax evaders, business cheats and those who wasted food, who are ground between millstones or forced to embrace red-hot metal pillars. The SIXTH COURT under Biancheng Wang punishes those who desecrated sacred texts, complained against heaven, misused religious teachings or showed disrespect to the Sanbao, and its sinners are sawn in half or crushed under boulders. The SEVENTH COURT under Taishan Wang deals with grave robbers, those who sold or consumed human flesh, and violators of burial customs, whose punishments include being steamed alive or having the intestines pulled out. The EIGHTH COURT, called Hot Suffocation Hell, is ruled by Dushi Wang and judges crimes of filial impiety, with sixteen sub-hells for those who caused their parents suffering; King Dushi also sentences cheating during examinations and harming others to benefit oneself. The NINTH COURT, called Iron Web, is ruled by Pingdeng Wang, who is the final checkpoint before rebirth: his job is to catch anything the previous eight courts missed, reviewing the entire case file and checking for clerical errors. The TENTH COURT, the Wheel of Rebirth, is ruled by Zhuanlun Wang, literally 'King of the Turning Wheel', who does not punish anyone but assigns the next life; Lady Meng Po gives the Tea of Forgetfulness there, which erases the memory of the punishments, and souls then pass through the Wheel of Reincarnation to be reborn as human or animal according to the past life."

      "reference": "Reference treatment of the Ten Courts of Diyu: the concept of the Ten Courts of Yanluo began after Chinese folk religion was influenced by Buddhism; hell is divided into ten courts, each ruled by a king who judges the dead for their earthly sins before sentencing punishment, and the ten are collectively the Ten Yama Kings, operating like imperial judges supported by clerks who maintain detailed registers of human actions. They are, in order: the first hall's King Qinguang, the second hall's King Chujiang, the third hall's King Songdi, the fourth hall's King Wuguan, the fifth hall's King Yama (Yanluo Wang), the sixth hall's King Biancheng, the seventh hall's King Taishan, the eighth hall's King Dushi, the ninth hall's King Pingdeng, and the tenth hall's King of the Turning Wheel (Zhuanlun Wang). Yanluo Wang rules the Fifth Court and is the most famous of the ten."
     }
    ],
    "notes": "Reciprocated within this batch. THE TEN ARE A SINGLE INSTITUTION, not ten unrelated judges: a soul passes through the courts in order, and each king's jurisdiction is defined by what the others do not handle."

   {
    "claim": "King of the seventh Court of Diyu, who judges grave robbers, those who sold or consumed human flesh, and violators of burial customs; the court's punishment is being steamed alive or having the intestines pulled out.",
    "citations": [
     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the individual Courts of Diyu: the SECOND COURT under Chujiang Wang punishes corrupt officials, dishonest doctors and those who harmed others through deception, and its sinners face the Mountain of Ice, where they freeze in eternal cold. The THIRD COURT under Songdi Wang deals with ungrateful children, those who disrespected elders, and people who incited discord within families, and its punishment involves having one's heart torn out repeatedly, symbolising the pain they caused their families. The FOURTH COURT under Wuguan Wang punishes tax evaders, business cheats and those who wasted food, who are ground between millstones or forced to embrace red-hot metal pillars. The SIXTH COURT under Biancheng Wang punishes those who desecrated sacred texts, complained against heaven, misused religious teachings or showed disrespect to the Sanbao, and its sinners are sawn in half or crushed under boulders. The SEVENTH COURT under Taishan Wang deals with grave robbers, those who sold or consumed human flesh, and violators of burial customs, whose punishments include being steamed alive or having the intestines pulled out. The EIGHTH COURT, called Hot Suffocation Hell, is ruled by Dushi Wang and judges crimes of filial impiety, with sixteen sub-hells for those who caused their parents suffering; King Dushi also sentences cheating during examinations and harming others to benefit oneself. The NINTH COURT, called Iron Web, is ruled by Pingdeng Wang, who is the final checkpoint before rebirth: his job is to catch anything the previous eight courts missed, reviewing the entire case file and checking for clerical errors. The TENTH COURT, the Wheel of Rebirth, is ruled by Zhuanlun Wang, literally 'King of the Turning Wheel', who does not punish anyone but assigns the next life; Lady Meng Po gives the Tea of Forgetfulness there, which erases the memory of the punishments, and souls then pass through the Wheel of Reincarnation to be reborn as human or animal according to the past life."
     }

    "sources": [
     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the individual Courts of Diyu: the SECOND COURT under Chujiang Wang punishes corrupt officials, dishonest doctors and those who harmed others through deception, and its sinners face the Mountain of Ice, where they freeze in eternal cold. The THIRD COURT under Songdi Wang deals with ungrateful children, those who disrespected elders, and people who incited discord within families, and its punishment involves having one's heart torn out repeatedly, symbolising the pain they caused their families. The FOURTH COURT under Wuguan Wang punishes tax evaders, business cheats and those who wasted food, who are ground between millstones or forced to embrace red-hot metal pillars. The SIXTH COURT under Biancheng Wang punishes those who desecrated sacred texts, complained against heaven, misused religious teachings or showed disrespect to the Sanbao, and its sinners are sawn in half or crushed under boulders. The SEVENTH COURT under Taishan Wang deals with grave robbers, those who sold or consumed human flesh, and violators of burial customs, whose punishments include being steamed alive or having the intestines pulled out. The EIGHTH COURT, called Hot Suffocation Hell, is ruled by Dushi Wang and judges crimes of filial impiety, with sixteen sub-hells for those who caused their parents suffering; King Dushi also sentences cheating during examinations and harming others to benefit oneself. The NINTH COURT, called Iron Web, is ruled by Pingdeng Wang, who is the final checkpoint before rebirth: his job is to catch anything the previous eight courts missed, reviewing the entire case file and checking for clerical errors. The TENTH COURT, the Wheel of Rebirth, is ruled by Zhuanlun Wang, literally 'King of the Turning Wheel', who does not punish anyone but assigns the next life; Lady Meng Po gives the Tea of Forgetfulness there, which erases the memory of the punishments, and souls then pass through the Wheel of Reincarnation to be reborn as human or animal according to the past life."

      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the individual Courts of Diyu: the SECOND COURT under Chujiang Wang punishes corrupt officials, dishonest doctors and those who harmed others through deception, and its sinners face the Mountain of Ice, where they freeze in eternal cold. The THIRD COURT under Songdi Wang deals with ungrateful children, those who disrespected elders, and people who incited discord within families, and its punishment involves having one's heart torn out repeatedly, symbolising the pain they caused their families. The FOURTH COURT under Wuguan Wang punishes tax evaders, business cheats and those who wasted food, who are ground between millstones or forced to embrace red-hot metal pillars. The SIXTH COURT under Biancheng Wang punishes those who desecrated sacred texts, complained against heaven, misused religious teachings or showed disrespect to the Sanbao, and its sinners are sawn in half or crushed under boulders. The SEVENTH COURT under Taishan Wang deals with grave robbers, those who sold or consumed human flesh, and violators of burial customs, whose punishments include being steamed alive or having the intestines pulled out. The EIGHTH COURT, called Hot Suffocation Hell, is ruled by Dushi Wang and judges crimes of filial impiety, with sixteen sub-hells for those who caused their parents suffering; King Dushi also sentences cheating during examinations and harming others to benefit oneself. The NINTH COURT, called Iron Web, is ruled by Pingdeng Wang, who is the final checkpoint before rebirth: his job is to catch anything the previous eight courts missed, reviewing the entire case file and checking for clerical errors. The TENTH COURT, the Wheel of Rebirth, is ruled by Zhuanlun Wang, literally 'King of the Turning Wheel', who does not punish anyone but assigns the next life; Lady Meng Po gives the Tea of Forgetfulness there, which erases the memory of the punishments, and souls then pass through the Wheel of Reincarnation to be reborn as human or animal according to the past life."
     }

    "weight": "high"

      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the individual Courts of Diyu: the SECOND COURT under Chujiang Wang punishes corrupt officials, dishonest doctors and those who harmed others through deception, and its sinners face the Mountain of Ice, where they freeze in eternal cold. The THIRD COURT under Songdi Wang deals with ungrateful children, those who disrespected elders, and people who incited discord within families, and its punishment involves having one's heart torn out repeatedly, symbolising the pain they caused their families. The FOURTH COURT under Wuguan Wang punishes tax evaders, business cheats and those who wasted food, who are ground between millstones or forced to embrace red-hot metal pillars. The SIXTH COURT under Biancheng Wang punishes those who desecrated sacred texts, complained against heaven, misused religious teachings or showed disrespect to the Sanbao, and its sinners are sawn in half or crushed under boulders. The SEVENTH COURT under Taishan Wang deals with grave robbers, those who sold or consumed human flesh, and violators of burial customs, whose punishments include being steamed alive or having the intestines pulled out. The EIGHTH COURT, called Hot Suffocation Hell, is ruled by Dushi Wang and judges crimes of filial impiety, with sixteen sub-hells for those who caused their parents suffering; King Dushi also sentences cheating during examinations and harming others to benefit oneself. The NINTH COURT, called Iron Web, is ruled by Pingdeng Wang, who is the final checkpoint before rebirth: his job is to catch anything the previous eight courts missed, reviewing the entire case file and checking for clerical errors. The TENTH COURT, the Wheel of Rebirth, is ruled by Zhuanlun Wang, literally 'King of the Turning Wheel', who does not punish anyone but assigns the next life; Lady Meng Po gives the Tea of Forgetfulness there, which erases the memory of the punishments, and souls then pass through the Wheel of Reincarnation to be reborn as human or animal according to the past life."

      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the Ten Courts of Diyu: the concept of the Ten Courts of Yanluo began after Chinese folk religion was influenced by Buddhism; hell is divided into ten courts, each ruled by a king who judges the dead for their earthly sins before sentencing punishment, and the ten are collectively the Ten Yama Kings, operating like imperial judges supported by clerks who maintain detailed registers of human actions. They are, in order: the first hall's King Qinguang, the second hall's King Chujiang, the third hall's King Songdi, the fourth hall's King Wuguan, the fifth hall's King Yama (Yanluo Wang), the sixth hall's King Biancheng, the seventh hall's King Taishan, the eighth hall's King Dushi, the ninth hall's King Pingdeng, and the tenth hall's King of the Turning Wheel (Zhuanlun Wang). Yanluo Wang rules the Fifth Court and is the most famous of the ten."

The THIRD COURT under Songdi Wang deals with ungrateful children, those who disrespected elders, and people who incited discord within families, and its punishment involves having one's heart torn out repeatedly, symbolising the pain they caused their families. The FOURTH COURT under Wuguan Wang punishes tax evaders, business cheats and those who wasted food, who are ground between millstones or forced to embrace red-hot metal pillars. The THIRD COURT under Songdi Wang deals with ungrateful children, those who disrespected elders, and people who incited discord within families, and its punishment involves having one's heart torn out repeatedly, symbolising the pain they caused their families. The FOURTH COURT under Wuguan Wang punishes tax evaders, business cheats and those who wasted food, who are ground between millstones or forced to embrace red-hot metal pillars. The SIXTH COURT under Biancheng Wang punishes those who desecrated sacred texts, complained against heaven, misused religious teachings or showed disrespect to the Sanbao, and its sinners are sawn in half or crushed under boulders. The SEVENTH COURT under Taishan Wang deals with grave robbers, those who sold or consumed human flesh, and violators of burial customs, whose punishments include being steamed alive or having the intestines pulled out. The EIGHTH COURT, called Hot Suffocation Hell, is ruled by Dushi Wang and judges crimes of filial impiety, with sixteen sub-hells for those who caused their parents suffering; King Dushi also sentences cheating during examinations and harming others to benefit oneself. The NINTH COURT, called Iron Web, is ruled by Pingdeng Wang, who is the final checkpoint before rebirth: his job is to catch anything the previous eight courts missed, reviewing the entire case file and checking for clerical errors. The TENTH COURT, the Wheel of Rebirth, is ruled by Zhuanlun Wang, literally 'King of the Turning Wheel', who does not punish anyone but assigns the next life; Lady Meng Po gives the Tea of Forgetfulness there, which erases the memory of the punishments, and souls then pass through the Wheel of Reincarnation to be reborn as human or animal according to the past life."
     }

================================================================================
BLOCK 3 — THE THREE MISSING DRAGON KINGS, AND THE BOY NEZHA KILLED
================================================================================

**Ao Qin** of the South Sea, **Ao Run** of the West, **Ao Shun** of the North —
brothers of the Ao Guang the corpus already held. A cardinal-direction set with
one direction in it is not a set, and the story the sources tell about them (all
four punished together by the Jade Emperor for bringing rain without permission
during a drought) needs all four to be a story.

**Ao Bing** is the third son of Ao Guang, and the corpus **held the killer and not
the killed**: Nezha, Li Jing, Jinzha and Muzha were all records, and the dragon
prince whose death sets the entire Nezha cycle in motion was not. He is typed
`demigod` — the computed tier, a deity father and an unnamed co-parent averaged,
not an assertion.

      "reference": "Reference treatment of the Four Dragon Kings: they are Ao Guang, Dragon King of the East Sea, Ao Qin of the South Sea, Ao Run of the West Sea and Ao Shun of the North Sea, and they are all siblings, Ao Guang being the oldest brother. Ao Guang rules the East Sea (the Yellow Sea), Ao Qin the South Sea (the South China Sea), Ao Run the West Sea (Qinghai Lake) and Ao Shun the North Sea (Lake Baikal); each is associated with one of the four cardinal directions. Together they serve under the Jade Emperor, carrying out celestial commands relating to rainfall, tides and maritime order, and they were punished by the Jade Emperor for defying the Heavenly court and bringing rain on Earth without permission during a drought. Ao Guang is associated with the element of wood and the spring season, his symbol the azure dragon."

================================================================================
BLOCK 4 — THE STORM QUARTET, THE THREE OFFICIALS, AND THE LOCAL GODS
================================================================================

**Dianmu, Yushi and Fengbo** complete the quartet that stands together in the
larger temples: thunder, lightning, rain and wind. Fengbo's record keeps both of
his forms rather than choosing — the Earl of Wind who works a goatskin bag, later
transformed in the tradition into Feng Po Po, Old Lady Wind.

**Tianguan, Diguan and Shuiguan**, the Three Officials, rank immediately below the
Jade Emperor, and the corpus held none of them. They cannot be authored singly
either: a confession of sins is written **in triplicate**, one copy burned for
heaven, one buried in earth and one sunk in water. The rite requires all three
destinations.

**Tudigong and Chenghuang** are the bottom and middle of the celestial
bureaucracy. Both are **offices rather than persons** — every locality has its own
Tudigong, every town its own City God, filled by a deified local worthy — and both
records say so. They are authored because the tradition treats the office itself
as a deity with a cult two millennia old.

Fengbo, also known as Fengshi, is the Taoist deity of the wind: roaring winds rush from a goatskin bag manipulated by Fengbo, the Earl of Wind, who was later transformed into Feng Po Po, Old Lady Wind. In larger temples and Daoist settings Leigong may appear alongside Dianmu, Fengbo and Yushi, together forming part of a wider celestial system governing storms, rain, wind, thunder and lightning."

     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the Chinese weather deities: Dianmu, also known as Leizi, is the goddess of lightning, said to use flashing mirrors to send bolts of lightning across the sky; she is the deity in charge of lightning, attributed to yin, hence called 'mu', mother or female, and also known as Goddess of Golden Light or Lightning Lady, and she is married to Leigong, the god of thunder. Leigong is the deity in charge of thunder, attributed to yang, hence called 'gong', lord or male. Yushi, the Rain Master, controls the rain and is said to dip his sword in a pail of water and let it drip down from the blade over the earth. Fengbo, also known as Fengshi, is the Taoist deity of the wind: roaring winds rush from a goatskin bag manipulated by Fengbo, the Earl of Wind, who was later transformed into Feng Po Po, Old Lady Wind. In larger temples and Daoist settings Leigong may appear alongside Dianmu, Fengbo and Yushi, together forming part of a wider celestial system governing storms, rain, wind, thunder and lightning."

    "startEvent": "One of the Sanguan, the Three Great Officials of Daoism: the Official of Heaven, who confers blessings and bestows happiness; three of the highest sky gods of Daoism, subordinate only to the Jade Emperor, whose feast falls on the fifteenth of the first month.",
    "sources": [
     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the Sanguan, the Three Officials of Daoism: Tianguan, the Heavenly Official who confers blessings and bestows happiness; Diguan, the Earthly Official who absolves sins and grants remission of them; and Shuiguan, the Water Official who eliminates misfortunes and averts them. They are three of the highest sky gods in Daoism, subordinate only to the Jade Emperor. On the fifteenth of the first month, the fifteenth of the seventh month and the fifteenth of the tenth month — the birthdays of the Three Officials of Heaven, Earth and Water — Daoists go to temples and burn incense to offer sacrifices. The Daoist principle held heaven, earth and water to be three transcendent powers, with confession of sins written in triplicate, one copy burned for heaven, another buried in earth and the third sunk in water. The Chinese theatre did much to popularise Tianguan by introducing a skit before each play called 'The Official of Heaven Brings Happiness'."

The Daoist principle held heaven, earth and water to be three transcendent powers, with confession of sins written in triplicate, one copy burned for heaven, another buried in earth and the third sunk in water. The Chinese theatre did much to popularise Tianguan by introducing a skit before each play called 'The Official of Heaven Brings Happiness'."

     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of Tudigong and the City God: Tudigong, 'Lord of the Soil and the Ground', is a tutelary deity of natural locality who guards the land's natural environment, animals and plants as well as humans; a god of the LOWEST RANK, he receives wishes from worshippers and grants them according to their deeds, and the definitive characteristic of Tudigongs is that they are limited to their specific geographical locations. A City God (Chenghuang) is a tutelary deity believed to protect the people and affairs of a particular village, town or city, and the corresponding location in the afterlife; City God cults appeared over two millennia ago and originally involved worship of a protective deity of a town's walls and moats, the term later being applied to deified leaders from the town who serve in authority over the souls of its deceased and intervene in the affairs of the living, in conjunction with other officials of the hierarchy of divine beings. City Gods rank ABOVE tudigong, which themselves rank above landlord deities."

      "reference": "Reference treatment of Tudigong and the City God: Tudigong, 'Lord of the Soil and the Ground', is a tutelary deity of natural locality who guards the land's natural environment, animals and plants as well as humans; a god of the LOWEST RANK, he receives wishes from worshippers and grants them according to their deeds, and the definitive characteristic of Tudigongs is that they are limited to their specific geographical locations. A City God (Chenghuang) is a tutelary deity believed to protect the people and affairs of a particular village, town or city, and the corresponding location in the afterlife; City God cults appeared over two millennia ago and originally involved worship of a protective deity of a town's walls and moats, the term later being applied to deified leaders from the town who serve in authority over the souls of its deceased and intervene in the affairs of the living, in conjunction with other officials of the hierarchy of divine beings. City Gods rank ABOVE tudigong, which themselves rank above landlord deities."
     }
    ],
    "notes": "Reciprocated within this batch."
   }
  ],
  "cultRecords": [],
  "sources": [
   {
    "claim": "The City God: a tutelary deity who protects the people and affairs of a particular village, town or city and the corresponding place in the afterlife; originally the protective deity of a town's walls and moats, the office later filled by deified leaders of the town, who hold authority over the souls of its dead and intervene in the affairs of the living.",

    "weight": "high"
   }
  ],
  "notes": "AN OFFICE, NOT A PERSON, AND THE RECORD SAYS SO: every town has its own City God, and the post is filled by a deified local worthy. It is authored because the tradition treats the office itself as a deity with a cult two millennia old — the same reasoning that admits Tudigong."
 }
]
```

================================================================================
BLOCK 5 — THE FOLK AND TAOIST FIGURES
================================================================================

**Zhinü and Niulang** are the whole of Qixi, the most widely observed romantic
festival in the Chinese calendar, and the corpus held neither. The story is not
softened here: he takes her clothes so that she cannot leave. **Zhinü's era is set
to her father's**, `mythic-prehistory`, rather than the `mythic` her story would
otherwise take — the registry's era ordering puts `mythic` BEFORE
`mythic-prehistory`, so authoring her in `mythic` would have made a daughter older
than her father and tripped the era-inversion check.

**Yue Lao** keeps a marital register, which is the point: marriage in this
pantheon is an administrative act, filed and bound with red thread, exactly as
judgement is in Diyu and blessing is under the Three Officials.

**Bixia Yuanjun** is the goddess of Mount Tai, and the corpus held the *god* of
Mount Tai without her. **No parentage is asserted**: the tradition commonly makes
her Dongyue Dadi's daughter, but this pass's research did not return that
filiation, so the link records only the shared mountain cult the sources do
attest.

**Taiyi Zhenren** is Nezha's master — the corpus held the pupil and not the
teacher. **Lu Ban**, **Wu Gang**, **Kui Xing**, **Houtu**, **Tai Sui**, and the
door gods **Shenshu and Yulü** close the batch. Tai Sui is authored **as the
office**, not as the sixty celestial generals who rotate through it; the sixty
are censused REJECT as a class.

Qixi is celebrated on the seventh night of the seventh lunar month, when a bridge of magpies forms across the sky for the lovers to cross.     "claim": "The old lunar matchmaker, the Taoist deity in charge of the red thread and of marriages, represented with a staff in his right hand, a marital register in his left, and red thread draped over his arms.",
    "citations": [
     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of Zhinü, Niulang and Yue Lao: Niulang the Cowherd and Zhinü the Weaver Girl are the central figures of the Qixi story; Zhinü is a goddess skilled at weaving colourful clouds, the daughter of the Jade Emperor and the goddess of weaving, whose star is Vega, while Niulang is the star Altair in Aquila. Zhinü came down to Earth and, while bathing in a river, met Niulang; he stole her clothes, and unable to return to heaven without them she decided to marry him. When the heavenly queen found out about their union she tore them apart, condemning them to live on opposite sides of the Milky Way. Qixi is celebrated on the seventh night of the seventh lunar month, when a bridge of magpies forms across the sky for the lovers to cross. Yue Xia Laoren, the old lunar matchmaker god, is the Taoist deity in charge of the red thread and of marriages, typically represented with a staff in his right hand, a marital register in his left, and red thread draped over his arms."
     }
    ],
    "weight": "high"
   }
  ],
  "notes": "HE KEEPS A REGISTER, WHICH IS THE POINT: marriage in this pantheon is an administrative act, filed and bound with thread, exactly as judgement is in Diyu and as blessing is under the Three Officials. The corpus held the bureaucracy of death and none of the bureaucracy of marriage."

    "sources": [
     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of Lu Ban, Wu Gang, Kui Xing, Houtu and Tai Sui: Lu Ban is the god of carpentry and furniture and the patron saint of builders and contractors, an innovator and engineer credited with inventions such as the saw and the shovel. Wu Gang is associated with the phrase 'pluck osmanthus in the Toad Palace', a chengyu connecting the lunar tree with passing the imperial examinations; the tree is now known in Chinese as the yuegui, the Moon gui. Kui Xing is a brilliant but ugly dwarf who, as the god of examinations, became the deity of scholars who took the imperial examinations; he is often depicted holding a writing brush to check off the most outstanding candidate, an official seal in the other hand, and balancing a bushel basket to measure the talents of examinees. Houtu, also Houtu Niangniang, the Earth Queen Lady, otherwise Dimu or Dimu Niangniang, Lady Mother Earth, is the deity of all land and earth in Chinese religion. In Chinese astrology Tai Sui means the Guardian God of the Year and governs all the fortunes of a particular year; there are a total of sixty Tai Sui, each personified as a celestial general in service of the Jade Emperor."

     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of the Four Heavenly Ministers and the Jade Emperor's subordinates: the Four Heavenly Ministers are four of the highest sky deities of Daoism, subordinate only to the Great Jade Emperor, and they assist him in administering all phenomena of the universe; Houtu is responsible for all things on earth and is listed as one of them, presiding over the duality of yin and yang, the beauty of all things and the splendour of mountains and rivers. The Imperial God of Earth had a MALE form before the Tang dynasty and acquired a FEMALE form during Wu Zetian's reign, and because Tian, sky, represents yang and Di, earth, represents yin, most people hold Houtu to be female. Wu Gang was a woodcutter who aspired to immortality but, through impatience and lack of dedication, was PUNISHED BY THE JADE EMPEROR, who sent him to the Moon Palace to fell a massive osmanthus tree that heals itself at every stroke; he shares the moon with Chang'e and the jade rabbit, THOUGH THEIR STORIES ARE ONLY LOOSELY CONNECTED. The sixty Tai Sui are each personified as a celestial general IN SERVICE OF the Jade Emperor, and the Three Officials are subordinate only to him."

    "claim": "The woodcutter of the moon, whose endless felling of the lunar osmanthus gives the phrase 'to pluck osmanthus in the Toad Palace' — a saying for passing the imperial examinations; the tree is the yuegui, the Moon gui.",
    "citations": [
     {
      "kind": "secondary",
      "reference": "Reference treatment of Lu Ban, Wu Gang, Kui Xing, Houtu and Tai Sui: Lu Ban is the god of carpentry and furniture and the patron saint of builders and contractors, an innovator and engineer credited with inventions such as the saw and the shovel. Wu Gang is associated with the phrase 'pluck osmanthus in the Toad Palace', a chengyu connecting the lunar tree with passing the imperial examinations; the tree is now known in Chinese as the yuegui, the Moon gui. Kui Xing is a brilliant but ugly dwarf who, as the god of examinations, became the deity of scholars who took the imperial examinations; he is often depicted holding a writing brush to check off the most outstanding candidate, an official seal in the other hand, and balancing a bushel basket to measure the talents of examinees. Houtu, also Houtu Niangniang, the Earth Queen Lady, otherwise Dimu or Dimu Niangniang, Lady Mother Earth, is the deity of all land and earth in Chinese religion. In Chinese astrology Tai Sui means the Guardian God of the Year and governs all the fortunes of a particular year; there are a total of sixty Tai Sui, each personified as a celestial general in service of the Jade Emperor."
     }

Wu Gang was a woodcutter who aspired to immortality but, through impatience and lack of dedication, was PUNISHED BY THE JADE EMPEROR, who sent him to the Moon Palace to fell a massive osmanthus tree that heals itself at every stroke; he shares the moon with Chang'e and the jade rabbit, THOUGH THEIR STORIES ARE ONLY LOOSELY CONNECTED. The sixty Tai Sui are each personified as a celestial general IN SERVICE OF the Jade Emperor, and the Three Officials are subordinate only to him."

Wu Gang is associated with the phrase 'pluck osmanthus in the Toad Palace', a chengyu connecting the lunar tree with passing the imperial examinations; the tree is now known in Chinese as the yuegui, the Moon gui. Kui Xing is a brilliant but ugly dwarf who, as the god of examinations, became the deity of scholars who took the imperial examinations; he is often depicted holding a writing brush to check off the most outstanding candidate, an official seal in the other hand, and balancing a bushel basket to measure the talents of examinees. Houtu, also Houtu Niangniang, the Earth Queen Lady, otherwise Dimu or Dimu Niangniang, Lady Mother Earth, is the deity of all land and earth in Chinese religion. In Chinese astrology Tai Sui means the Guardian God of the Year and governs all the fortunes of a particular year; there are a total of sixty Tai Sui, each personified as a celestial general in service of the Jade Emperor."

================================================================================
WHAT WAS NOT INGESTED, AND WHY
================================================================================

**BLOCKED (5)** — genuine blockers, with the exact uncertainty recorded:

  Yutu            the jade rabbit of the moon. Searched in this pass alongside Wu
                  Gang and NOT RETURNED; the corpus already holds Chang'e, so the
                  lunar cast is half-filled and the remainder is a known gap.
  Jiutian Xuannü  the Mysterious Lady of the Nine Heavens. The search returned
                  nothing usable about her role or attestation.
  Wang Lingguan   the Taoist guardian marshal. Same: named in the query, absent
                  from the results.
  Sun Simiao /    the medicine king. NOT RESEARCHED in this pass rather than
  Yaowang         researched and found thin, and the row says which it is.
  Yin Jiao        identified in Fengshen Yanyi with Tai Sui. The identification
                  surfaced only as a link in the results, with no content, so
                  whether he is a separate figure or an alias of the Tai Sui
                  office is unresolved — and getting that wrong would create
                  exactly the alias-shadow defect lanes 29-33 spent five lanes
                  repairing.

**REJECT (5)** — out of scope by standing rule:

  Marshal Tianpeng          AN ALIAS OF A RECORD THE CORPUS ALREADY HOLDS. It is
                            Zhu Bajie's rank before his fall, and the sweep
                            resolved it to chinese_zhu_bajie.
  the sixty Tai Sui         a CLASS of sixty celestial generals rotating through
                            one office. The OFFICE is authored; the sixty are not.
  the Eighteen Levels of    a set of PLACES, not figures. The kings who preside
  Hell                      over the courts are authored instead.
  the Twenty-Eight Mansions a class of asterisms.
  huli jing (fox spirits)   a creature class — the standing rule that rejected the
                            nanchon, the Gede, the nagas, the tsen, the zaedtae
                            and the jinn.

**No image was ingested in this batch**, so the image-licensing rule is not
engaged.

================================================================================
VERIFICATION NOTES
================================================================================

  * **Two records carry parentIds, and both were checked against the registry's
    tier arithmetic AND its era ordering before authoring**: Ao Bing (Ao Guang,
    deity 1.0, halved = 0.5 -> demigod; era `mythic`, same as his father) and
    Zhinü (the Jade Emperor, deity 1.0, halved = 0.5 -> demigod; era
    `mythic-prehistory`, HER FATHER'S ERA, because `mythic` sorts earlier and
    would have inverted the generations). The other thirty author no parentIds.
  * **Chinese script is supplied for thirty-one of thirty-two records.** The one
    exception is Wuguan Wang, whose first element is written both 五官 and 仵官
    with no settled form, so the field is left EMPTY rather than a choice being
    made silently — and his etymology note says exactly that.
  * Symmetric relations inside the batch are reciprocated: the eight kings to each
    other, the three dragon brothers to each other, the three Officials, the storm
    quartet's in-batch members, Zhinü/Niulang, Tudigong/Chenghuang and the two
    door gods. Every link to a PRE-EXISTING record — Qinguang, Yanluo, Meng Po,
    Ao Guang, Nezha, Leigong, the Jade Emperor, Dongyue Dadi and Wenchang — uses a
    NON-SYMMETRIC kind written from this end only, so no pre-existing record is
    mutated.
  * **Two genuinely symmetric facts are recorded with non-symmetric kinds on
    purpose**: the three dragon brothers' link to Ao Guang uses
    `of-the-four-dragon-kings-with` rather than `sibling`, and Dianmu's link to
    Leigong uses `wife-of`. Both targets are pre-existing records this lane does
    not mutate, so a symmetric kind would leave a one-way symmetric relation
    dangling — `validate-transcripts` flagged exactly that on the first pass and
    the kinds were changed rather than the warning accepted. The brotherhood and
    the marriage are stated in each record's claim and notes instead.
  * This batch adds a LOT of edges on purpose. Lane 36 tripped the corpus's
    no-family-links ceiling by authoring set-members without writing the set
    relation that binds them; every set in this batch is bound from the start.
